In effect, the school created a type but not a will. Four years, if successful, resulted in an autobiographical blank, a mind on which only a water-mark had been stamped.

The stamp, as such things went, was a good one. The chief wonder of education is that it does not ruin everybody concerned in it. Sometimes in after life Adams debated whether it had not ruined him and most of his companions, but Harvard was probably less hurtful than any other university then in existence. It taught little, and that little ill, but it left the mind open, free from bias, ignorant of facts, but docile; supple, ready to receive knowledge.

What most disappointed the boy was the little he got from his mates. Speaking exactly, he got less than nothing, a result common enough in education. The Class of 1858 shows a list rather distinguished in their time—Alexander Agassiz and Phillips Brooks led it; H. H. Richardson and O. W. Holmes helped to close it—yet as help towards education he got nothing whatever from them or they from him until long after they had left college. Accident counts for much in companionship as in marriage; boys brought up together under like conditions have nothing to give each other. The Class of 1858, to which he belonged, was a typical collection of young New Englanders, quietly penetrating and aggressively commonplace; free from meannesses, jealousies, intrigues, enthusiasms, and passions; singularly indifferent to display; distrustful of themselves, and little disposed to trust any one else; with not much humor of their own, but readiness to enjoy that of others; negative to a degree that in the long run became positive and triumphant. Not harsh, rather liberal and open-minded, they were still as a body the most formidable critics one would care to meet. They never flattered, seldom praised; their attitude was a law of nature; their judgment beyond appeal, not an act either of intellect or emotion or of will, but a sort of gravitation. This was Harvard College incarnate, and the Class of 1858 somewhat extreme.

Of unity this band of nearly a hundred young men had no keen sense, but little energy of repulsion. They were pleasant to live with; chiefly because each individual appeared satisfied to stand alone. It seemed a sign of force; yet to stand alone is quite natural when one has no passions; still easier when one has no pains.

Into this dissolvent medium, chance enlarged Henry Adams’s education by tossing a trio of Virginians as little fitted for it as Sioux Indians to a treadmill. By further affinity, they fell into relation with the Bostonians, and in the end with Adams himself, though they and he knew how thin an edge of friendship separated them in 1856 from mortal enmity. One was the son of Colonel Robert E. Lee; the two others were town-Virginians from Petersburg. A fourth outsider came from Cincinnati and was half Kentuckian. For the first time his education brought him in contact with new types and taught him their values. He saw the New England type measure itself with another and was part of the process.

Lee, known through life as “Roony,” was a Virginian of the eighteenth century, much as Adams was a Bostonian of the same age. Tall, largely built, handsome, genial, with liberal Virginian openness, he had also the habit of command and took leadership as his natural habit. No one cared to contest it; none of the New Englanders wanted command. For a year, at least, Lee was the most popular and prominent young man in his class, but then slowly dropped into the background. The habit of command was not enough, and the Virginian had little else. He was simple beyond analysis; so simple that even the simple New England student could not realize him. No one knew enough to know how ignorant he was; how childlike; how helpless before the relative complexity of a school. As an animal, the Southerner seemed to have every advantage, but even as an animal he steadily lost ground.

The lesson in education was vital to these young men, who, within ten years, killed each other by scores in the act of testing their college conclusions. Strictly, the Southerner had no mind; he had temperament. He was not a scholar; he had no intellectual training; he could not analyze an idea, and he could not even conceive of admitting two; but in life one could get on very well without ideas, if one had only the social instinct. Dozens of eminent statesmen were men of Lee’s type and maintained themselves in the legislature, but college was a sharper test. The habits of neither section were good; both were apt to drink hard and to live low lives; but the Bostonian suffered less than the Virginian. Commonly the Bostonian could take some care of himself even in his worst stages, while the Virginian became quarrelsome and dangerous. When a Virginian had brooded a few days over an imaginary grief and substantial whiskey, none of his Northern friends could be sure that he might not be waiting, round the corner, with a knife or pistol, to revenge insult by the dry light of delirium tremens; and when things reached this condition, Lee had to exhaust his authority over his own staff. Lee was a gentleman of the old school; he kept his temper and his friends under control.

Adams liked the Virginians. No one was more obnoxious to them by name and prejudice; yet their friendship was unbroken and even warm. At a moment when the immediate future posed no problem so vital as the relative energy and endurance of North and South, this contact with Southern character was a sort of education for its own sake. No doubt the self-esteem of the Yankee, which tended to self-distrust, was flattered by gaining the slow conviction that the Southerner, with his slave-owning limitations, was as little fit to succeed in the struggle of modern life as though he were still a maker of stone axes, living in caves, and hunting the bos primigenius, and that every quality in which he was strong, made him weaker; but Adams began to fear that even in this respect one eighteenth-century type might not differ deeply from another. Roony Lee had changed little from the Virginian of a century before; but Adams was himself a good deal nearer the type of his great-grandfather than to that of a railway superintendent. He was little more fit than the Virginians to deal with a future America which showed no fancy for the past. Already Northern society betrayed a preference for economists over diplomats or soldiers—one might even call it a jealousy—against which two eighteenth-century types had little chance to live, and which they had in common to fear.

Nothing short of this curious sympathy could have brought into close relations two young men so hostile as Lee and Adams; but the chief difference between them as collegians consisted only in scholarship: Lee was a total failure; Adams a partial one. Lee felt his failure more sensibly, and gladly seized escape by accepting a commission offered him by General Winfield Scott in the force being organized against the Mormons. He asked Adams to write his letter of acceptance, which flattered Adams’s vanity more than any Northern compliment; in days of violent bitterness it showed good temper. The diplomat felt his profession.

If the student got little from his mates, he got little more from his masters. The four years passed at college were, for his purposes, wasted. He did not want to be one in a hundred—one per cent of an education. He regarded himself as the only person for whom his education had value, and wanted the whole of it. He got barely half an average. Long afterwards, when life led him back to teach in his turn what no student naturally cared or needed to know, he looked up his record and found himself graded precisely in the middle. In the one branch he most needed—mathematics—failure was so nearly universal that grading had little value. At best he could never have been a mathematician; at worst he would never have cared to be one; but he needed to read mathematics, like any other universal language, and he never reached the alphabet.

Beyond two or three Greek plays, he got nothing from the ancient languages. Beyond some incoherent theories of free-trade and protection, he got little from Political Economy. He did not remember to have heard the name of Karl Marx or the title of “Capital.” He was equally ignorant of Auguste Comte. These were the two writers of his time who most influenced its thought. The bit of practical teaching he afterwards reviewed with most curiosity was Chemistry, which taught him a number of theories that befogged his mind for a lifetime. The only teaching that appealed to his imagination was a course by Louis Agassiz on the Glacial Period and Paleontology, which had more influence on his curiosity than the rest of the instruction altogether. The entire work of the four years could have been easily put into the work of any four months in after life.

Harvard was a negative force, and negative forces have value. Slowly it weakened the violent political bias of childhood, not by putting interests in its place, but by mental habits which had no bias at all. It would also have weakened the literary bias, had he been capable of finding other amusement, but the climate kept him steady to desultory and useless reading, till he had run through libraries whose titles he forgot. Rather by instinct than by guidance, he turned to writing, and his professors or tutors occasionally gave his English composition a hesitating approval; but even when he made a long struggle for recognition, he never convinced his teachers that his abilities warranted placing him among the first third of his class. Instructors generally reach a fairly accurate gauge of their scholars’ powers. He himself held the opinion that his instructors were nearly right; later, as professor, after making mortifying mistakes in ranking his own scholars, he still insisted that on the whole he was not far wrong. Student or professor, he accepted the negative standard because it was the standard of the school.

From the first he wanted to be done with it, and stood watching vaguely for a path and a direction. The world seemed large, but the paths that led into it were not many and lay mostly through Boston, where he did not want to go. By pure chance the first door of escape that seemed to offer a hope led into Germany, and James Russell Lowell opened it.

Lowell, on succeeding Longfellow as Professor of Belles-Lettres, had duly gone to Germany and brought back whatever he found to bring. The literary world then agreed that truth survived in Germany alone, and Carlyle, Matthew Arnold, Renan, Emerson, with scores of followers, taught the German faith. The literary world had revolted against the yoke of coming capitalism—its money-lenders, bank directors, and railway magnates. Thackeray and Dickens followed Balzac in scratching and biting the middle class with savage ill-temper. The satirists and idealists seized the press and, as they thought the Second Empire a disgrace to France and a danger to England, turned to Germany because at that moment Germany was neither economical nor military, and a hundred years behind western Europe in the simplicity of its standard. German thought, method, honesty, and even taste became the standards of scholarship. Goethe was raised to the rank of Shakespeare—Kant ranked as a law-giver above Plato. All serious scholars were obliged to become German, for German thought was revolutionizing criticism. Lowell had followed, not very enthusiastically, but with sufficient conviction, and invited his scholars to join him. Adams accepted the invitation, rather for Lowell than for Germany, but in good faith. It was the first serious attempt he had made to direct his own education; he was sure of getting some education out of it; not perhaps what he expected, but at least a path.

Singularly circuitous and excessively wasteful of energy the path proved to be, but he could never see what other was open to him; he could have done no better had he foreseen every stage of his coming life, and would probably have done worse. The preliminary step was pure gain. Lowell had brought back the habit of allowing students to read with him privately in his study. Adams asked the privilege, and used it to read a little, and to talk a great deal, for the personal contact pleased and flattered him, as that of older men ought to flatter and please the young. Lowell was a new element in the boy’s life. As practical a New Englander as any, he leaned towards the Concord faith rather than Boston; for Concord, in the dark days of 1856, glowed with pure light. Adams approached it much as he would have entered a Gothic Cathedral, for he knew the priests regarded him as only a worm. To the Concord Church all Adamses were minds of dust and emptiness, devoid of feeling, poetry or imagination; little higher than the common scourings of State Street. Already, at eighteen, he had begun to feel uncertainty about so many matters more important than Adamses that his mind rebelled against no discipline merely personal; he was ready to admit his unworthiness if only he might penetrate the shrine. He was slipping away from fixed principles; from Mount Vernon Street; from Quincy; from the eighteenth century; and his first steps led toward Concord.

He never reached Concord, and to Concord Church he, like the rest of mankind who accepted a material universe, remained always an insect—or something much lower—a man. It was surely no fault of his that the universe seemed to him real; perhaps—as Mr. Emerson justly said—it was so. In spite of a lifetime’s effort, he fell back into the heresy that if anything universal was unreal, it was himself and not the appearances; it was the poet and not the banker; his own thought, not the thing that moved it. He did not lack the wish to be transcendental. Concord seemed to him, at one time, more real than Quincy; yet in truth Russell Lowell was as little transcendental as Beacon Street. From him the boy got no revolutionary thought—objective or subjective as they used to call it—but good-humored encouragement to do what amused him, which consisted in passing two years in Europe after finishing the four at Cambridge.

The result seemed small in proportion to the effort, but it was the only positive result he could ever trace to the influence of Harvard, and he doubted whether Harvard influenced even that. Negative results in plenty he could trace, but he tended towards negation on his own account, as one side of the New England mind had always done; even there he could never feel sure that Harvard had more than reflected a weakness. In his opinion the education was not serious; hardly any Boston student took it seriously; none was sure that President Walker himself, or President Felton after him, took it more seriously than the students. For them all, the college offered chiefly advantages vulgarly called social, rather than mental.

Unluckily for this boy, social advantages were his only capital in life. Of money he had not much, of mind not more, but he could be certain that, barring his own faults, his social position would never be questioned. What he needed was a career in which social position had value. Never would he have to explain who he was; never need acquaintance to strengthen his standing; but he needed some one to show how to use the acquaintance he cared to make. He made no acquaintance in college which proved to have the smallest use in after life. All his Boston friends he knew before; contact of Bostonian with Bostonian was the last education they needed. Cordial as their relations were, they all flew off in different directions the moment they took their degrees. Harvard remained a tie, but a tie little stronger than Beacon Street and not so strong as State Street. A stranger like H. H. Richardson, who came from far New Orleans, and had his career before him to chase rather than to guide, might make valuable friendships; certainly Adams made none that he valued more in after life than Richardson, but the college relation had little to do with the later friendship. Life is a narrow valley, and the roads run close together. He would have attached himself to Richardson in any case, as he attached himself to others not at Harvard. The valley of life grew more narrow with years, and men with common tastes were bound to come together. He only knew that he would have felt himself on a more equal footing had he been less ignorant, and had he not thrown away ten years in acquiring what he might have acquired in one.

Socially or intellectually, the college was for him negative and in some ways mischievous. The most tolerant man of the world could not see good in the lower habits of the students, but the vices were less harmful than the virtues. The habit of drinking may have done no great harm; but the habit of looking at life as a social relation—an affair of society—did no good. It cultivated a weakness which needed no cultivation. If it had helped to make men of the world, or given the manners and instincts of any profession—temper, patience, courtesy, a faculty of profiting by the social defects of opponents—it would have been education worth more than mathematics or languages; but so far as it helped to make anything, it helped only to make the college standard permanent through life. The Bostonian educated at Harvard remained a collegian, if he stuck only to what the college gave. If parents went on, generation after generation, sending their children to Harvard for its social advantages, they perpetuated an inferior social type, as ill-fitted as the Oxford type for success in the next generation.

Luckily the old social standard, as President Walker or James Russell Lowell still showed it, was admirable, and if it had little practical value or personal influence on the mass, at least it preserved the tradition for those who liked it. The Harvard graduate was neither American nor European, nor even wholly Yankee; his admirers were few, his critics many; perhaps his worst weakness was his self-criticism and self-consciousness; but his ambitions, social or intellectual, were necessarily cheap even though negative. Afraid of serious risks, and still more afraid of ridicule, he seldom made a great failure of life, and nearly always led a life more or less worth living. So Henry Adams, well aware that he could not succeed as a scholar, and finding his social position beyond improvement or need of effort, betook himself to the single ambition that otherwise would scarcely have seemed a true outcome of the college, though it was the last remnant of the old Unitarian supremacy. He took to the pen. He wrote.

The College Magazine printed his work, and the Societies listened to his addresses. Lavish of praise the readers were not; the audiences listened in silence; but grave silence was the form of patience that meant possible future acceptance, and he went on writing. Soon he began to suffer from reaching his own limits. He could not be this—or that—or the other; always precisely the things he wanted to be. He had not wit or scope or force. Judges always ranked him beneath a rival, if he had any; and he believed the judges were right. His work seemed to him thin, commonplace, feeble. At best it showed only a feeling for form; an instinct of exclusion. Nothing shocked—not even its weakness.

Inevitably effort creates an ambition, and at that time the ambition of the literary student, which almost took the place of the regular prizes, was to be chosen Class Orator. This was political as well as literary success—precisely the eighteenth-century combination that fascinated an eighteenth-century boy. The idea lurked as a dream; he was outside the popular men. Year by year, his position seemed to improve, or rivals disappeared, until at last, to his astonishment, he found himself a candidate. The habits of the college permitted no active candidacy; he and his rivals had not a word to say for or against themselves; he was never consulted; he was not present at the proceedings; but it did happen that, one evening on returning from Boston, he received notice of his election, after a close contest, as Class Orator over the head of the first scholar, who was undoubtedly a better orator and a more popular man. In politics the success of the poorer candidate is common enough, and Henry Adams was a fairly trained politician, but he never understood how he defeated not only a more capable but a more popular rival.

To him the election seemed a miracle. This was no mock-modesty; he knew his rivals and their following as well as himself. What he did not know, even after four years, was Harvard College. He could never measure the bewildering impersonality of the men, who, at twenty, set no value either on official or personal standards. Here were nearly a hundred young men who had lived together intimately during four of the most impressionable years, and who, not only once but again and again, deliberately chose as their representatives precisely those of their companions who seemed least to represent them. As far as these Orators and Marshals had any position in a collegiate sense, it was that of indifference to the college. He never professed the smallest faith in universities of any kind; nor had he the faintest admiration for the university graduate; as a collegian he was known apart only by his habit of standing outside; and yet the singular fact remained that this commonplace body chose him to express his and their commonplaces. Secretly the successful candidate flattered himself—and them—with the hope that they might perhaps not be so commonplace as they thought; but this was only another proof that all were identical. They saw in him a representative—the kind they wanted—and he saw in them the most formidable array of judges he could ever meet, like so many mirrors of himself, an infinite reflection of his own shortcomings.

All the same, the choice was flattering; so flattering that it actually shocked his vanity, and would have shocked it more had he known it was to be the only flattery of the sort he was ever to receive. The function of Class Day was, in the eyes of nine-tenths, the most important of the college, and the figure of the Orator the most conspicuous. Unlike the Orators at regular Commencements, the Class Day Orator stood alone, or had only the Poet for rival. Crowded into the large church, the students, their families, friends, aunts, uncles and chaperones, attended with all the girls of sixteen or twenty, and there, in heat that might have melted bronze, they listened to an Orator and a Poet in gowns, reciting such platitudes as their experience and mild censors permitted. What he said he soon forgot, nor had it the least value for education; but he remembered what was said of it. One eminent relation remarked that, as the work of so young a man, the oration was singularly wanting in enthusiasm. The young man asked himself whether, rhetoric aside, this absence of enthusiasm was a defect or a merit, since it was all that Harvard taught, and all that the hundred young men, whom he tried to represent, expressed. Another comment threw more light. One elderly gentleman noticed the orator’s “perfect self-possession.”

Self-possession indeed! If Harvard gave nothing else, it gave calm. For four years each student had been obliged to figure daily before dozens who knew each other to the last fibre. One had done little but read papers to Societies, or act comedy in the Hasty Pudding, not to speak of regular exercises, and no audience in future life would ever be so intimately and terribly intelligent as these. Three-fourths of the graduates would rather have addressed the Council of Trent or the British Parliament than have acted Sir Anthony Absolute before a gala audience of the Hasty Pudding. Self-possession was the strongest part of Harvard, which certainly taught men to stand alone, so that nothing seemed stranger to its graduates than the paroxysms of terror before the public which often overcame the graduates of European universities.

Whether this was, or was not, education, Henry Adams never knew. He was ready to stand up before any audience in America or Europe, with nerves rather steadier for the excitement, but whether he should ever have anything to say, remained to be proved. As yet he knew nothing. Education had not begun.

A fourth child has the strength of his weakness. Being of no great value, he may throw himself away if he likes, and never be missed. Charles Francis Adams, the father, felt no love for Europe, which, as he and all the world agreed, unfitted Americans for America; yet a captious critic might have replied that all the success he or his father or his grandfather achieved was chiefly due to the field that Europe gave them. So much more timid are parents for their children than for themselves that Mr. and Mrs. Adams would have been content to see their children remain forever in Mount Vernon Street, could they have relied on the moral influences of Boston itself. The boy Henry wanted Europe; he observed conventions when he could not escape them; he seemed well-behaved when any one was looking at him; and what he was at heart no one could say, least of all himself. Therefore, when he presented to an exceedingly indulgent father and mother his request to begin at a German university the study of the Civil Law—although neither he nor they knew what the Civil Law was, or any reason for his studying it—the parents dutifully consented, and walked with him to the Quincy station to bid him good-bye, with a smile which he almost thought a tear.

Whether he deserved such indulgence, or was worth it, he knew as little as a professor at Harvard College; but whether worthy or not, he began his third or fourth attempt at education in November, 1858, by sailing on the steamer Persia, pride of Captain Judkins and the Cunard Line. Several of his college companions sailed with him, and the world looked cheerful until, on the third day, the world ran into a heavy storm. He learned then a lesson that stood by him better than any university teaching—the meaning of a November gale on the mid-Atlantic—which, for mere physical misery, passed endurance. He could never see the humor of sea-sickness; but the gale and the week's confusions made the first month of travel altogether the rapidest school of education he had yet found. One began to see that a great many impressions were needed to make very little education; how many could be crowded into one day without making any education at all became the pons asinorum of tourist mathematics.

The ocean, the Persia, Captain Judkins, and Mr. G. P. R. James, the most distinguished passenger, vanished in a furious gale in the Mersey to make place for the drearier picture of a Liverpool street from the Adelphi coffee-room in November murk, followed by the passionate delights of Chester and the romance of red-sandstone architecture. In days before tourists, when the romance was a reality, not a picture, the emotions were overwhelming. At Eaton Hall the boys were awed as Thackeray or Dickens would have felt in the presence of a Duke. Aristocracy was real. The long suite of lofty, gilded rooms; the portraits; the terraces and gardens; the sense of superiority in the England of the fifties, set the rich nobleman apart, above Americans and shopkeepers. Oliver Twist and Little Nell lurked in every churchyard shadow, alive. Even Charles the First was not very shadowy, standing on the tower to see his army defeated.

Then came the journey up through Birmingham and the Black District, a plunge into darkness lurid with flames; the sense of unknown horror in this weird gloom which then existed nowhere else, except in volcanic craters; the violent contrast between dense, smoky, impenetrable night and the soft green charm one glided into as one emerged. Karl Marx stood there in the mist, had he but known it; but the boy ran away from everything he disliked. He took the long, muddy, sordid, gas-lit dreariness of Oxford Street as another lesson that needed more to be rightly felt, and noticed one peculiarity: London was still London. Heavy, clumsy, arrogant, purse-proud, but not cheap; insular but large; absolutely self-confident. Boys in the street made such free comments on American clothes and figures that the travellers hurried to tall hats and long overcoats. No stranger had rights even in the Strand. History muttered down Fleet Street, like Dr. Johnson, in his ear; Vanity Fair was alive on Piccadilly in yellow chariots; blackened great houses bore funereal hatchments; and the most insolent structures in the world were the Royal Exchange and the Bank of England. He liked it best when he hated it.

Education began at the end, or perhaps would end at the beginning. Crossing to Antwerp, the Baron Osy steamed up the Scheldt through morning mists while a travelling band played and peasants dropped their tools to dance. Ostade and Teniers were alive; the thirteenth-century cathedral towered above a sixteenth-century mass of tiled roofs; the taste of the town was thick, rich, ripe, like a sweet wine—mediaeval so that Rubens seemed modern. He might as well have drunk out his excitement in old Malmsey, for all the education he got from it. One lesson he did learn, and immediately lost: he felt his middle ages and the sixteenth century alive. He was only too happy to feel himself kneeling at the foot of the Cross; he learned only to loathe the sordid necessity of getting up again and going about his stupid business.

Dropped into Berlin one morning without guide or direction, he floundered in a mere mess of misunderstandings. A student at twenty takes easily to anything, even to Berlin, and he would have accepted the thirteenth century pure and simple since his guides assured him this was the path; but a week's experience left him dazed and dull. Within a day or two he was running to beer-cellars and music-halls and dance-rooms, smoking bad tobacco, drinking poor beer, eating sauerkraut and sausages. This was easy. Trouble came when he asked for education. His friends registered him at the university; selected professors and courses; bought the Institutes of Gaius and German tomes on the Civil Law; and led him to his first lecture.

His first lecture was his last. He needed no more than one hour to see that he had made another failure in education, and this time a fatal one. That the language would require at least three months' hard work before he could touch the Law was annoying; but the shock that upset him was the university itself. Harvard had been torpid; Berlin was torpor made method. The Civil Law existed as lecture in its deadliest thirteenth-century form. The professor mumbled; the students made, or seemed to make, notes; they could have learned from books or discussion in a day more than they could learn from him in a month; yet to get a degree they must pay his fees, follow his course, be his scholars. To an American the result was worthless. Without some notion of the Common Law, the Civil Law failed him; and the student who knew enough Common Law had only to read the Pandects or commentators in America and be his own professor. Neither method nor matter nor manner could profit an American education.

This discovery shocked none of the students. They dutifully went to lectures, made notes, read textbooks, never taking the professor seriously. They were more serious in reading Heine. They were learning the language, they said. So the young man fell back on the language; being slow, he fell behind his friends; the gloom of a Berlin winter and Berlin architecture settled on his spirits like soot. One day on the Linden he caught sight of Charles Sumner in a cab and ran after him. They dined and went to hear William Tell. Sumner encouraged him: he had come to Berlin unable to say a word in the language; three months later he talked it to his cabman. Adams felt quite unable to attain such social advantages. Mr. Robert Apthorp, wintering in Berlin for its music, told how he had entered a public school and sat for months with ten-year-old boys, catching their phrases. The idea suited Adams's desperate mind. Mr. Apthorp took trouble to negotiate with the head-master of the Friedrichs-Wilhelm-Werdersches Gymnasium for permission to attend the Ober-tertia, a class of boys twelve or thirteen years old; there Adams went for three months as though he had not always avoided high schools. He never did anything else so foolish, but he was given a bit of education that served some purpose.

It was not merely the language, though three months in such fashion would teach a poodle enough to talk with a cabman; foreign students could expect no more, for they never by any chance would come in contact with German society, if society existed, about which they knew nothing. He thought himself quite Germanized in 1859 and deluded himself that he read as though it were English, which proved he knew little; but the success of his own experiment interested him less than contact with German education.

He had revolted at the American school and university; he had instantly rejected the German university; as a last experience he tried the German high school. In 1858 Berlin was a poor, keen-witted, provincial town, simple, dirty, uncivilized. Life was primitive beyond what an American boy could have imagined. Overridden by military methods and bureaucratic pettiness, Prussia was only beginning to free her hands. The future Kaiser Wilhelm I, regent for his insane brother Friedrich Wilhelm IV, seemed to pass his time looking at the passers-by from a palace window on the Linden. German manners, even at Court, were sometimes brutal; German thoroughness at school apt to be routine. Bismarck himself was struggling to begin a career against the inertia of the system. All earnest Germans were turning energies to reforming it from top to bottom; and Adams walked into a great public school to get educated at precisely the time when the Germans wanted most to get rid of the education they were forced to follow.

The head-master was excellent in his Prussian way; the instructors no worse than elsewhere; it was the system that struck the systemless American with horror. The arbitrary training given to the memory was stupefying; the strain it endured a form of torture; and the feats the boys performed without complaint pitiable. No other faculty than the memory seemed to be recognized. Least of all was any use made of reason, either analytic, synthetic, or dogmatic. The German government did not encourage reasoning.

All State education is a sort of dynamo machine for polarizing the popular mind; for turning and holding its lines of force in the direction supposed to be most effective for State purposes. The German machine was terribly efficient. Its effect on the children was pathetic. The school served the small tradesmen or bourgeoisie of the neighborhood; the children were Berliner-kinder if ever there were such, and of a class suspected of sympathy in the troubles of 1848. None was noble or connected with good society. Personally they were rather sympathetic; as objects of education they were proofs of nearly all the evils that a bad system could give. Their physique showed it first, though not wholly from school. German food was bad at best, and a diet of sauerkraut, sausage, and beer was never good; but it was not the food alone that made faces white and flesh flabby. They never breathed fresh air; they had never heard of a playground; in all Berlin not a cubic inch of oxygen was admitted in winter into an inhabited building; in school every room was tightly closed and had no ventilation; the air was foul beyond decency; and when the American opened a window in the five minutes between hours, he violated rules and was rebuked. If the boys had a holiday, they were apt to be taken on long tramps in the Thiergarten, ending in over-fatigue, tobacco-smoke, sausages, and beer. With this, they were required to prepare daily lessons that would have quickly broken down strong men of a healthy habit, and which they could learn only because their minds were morbid. The German university had seemed a failure, but the German high school was something very near an indictable nuisance.

Before April arrived, the experiment had reached its point. Nothing was left of education except the ghost of the Civil Law shut up in the darkest of closets, never to gibber again. The derisive Jew laughter of Heine ran through the university and everything else in Berlin. Of course, at twenty life is bound to be full, if only of Berlin beer; though small fragments remained of the education so promising—or promised—this is what most often happens when by-products turn out to be more valuable than staples.

German society, in an American sense, did not exist, or if it existed, never showed itself; the theatre was excellent; German opera, with the ballet, almost worth a journey; but the student's only clear gain—his single step to a higher life—came from time wasted; studies neglected; vices indulged; education reversed. It came from the despised beer-garden and music-hall, and it was accidental, unintended, unforeseen.

His companions insisted on passing two or three afternoons a week at music-halls, drinking beer, smoking German tobacco, looking at fat German women knitting, while an orchestra played dull music. Adams went for the sake of the company, with no pretence of enjoyment; when Mr. Apthorp gently protested that he exaggerated his indifference—for of course he enjoyed Beethoven—Adams replied simply that he loathed Beethoven, and felt a slight surprise when Mr. Apthorp and the others laughed as though they thought it humor. He saw no humor in it. He supposed that, except musicians, every one thought Beethoven a bore, as every one except mathematicians thought mathematics a bore. Sitting thus, mentally impassive, he was one day surprised to notice that his mind followed the movement of a Sinfonie. He could not have been more astonished had he suddenly read a new language. A prison-wall that barred his senses on one great side of life suddenly fell, of its own accord, without his knowing when it happened. Amid the fumes of coarse tobacco and poor beer, surrounded by the commonest of German Haus-frauen, a new sense burst out like a flower in his life, so superior to the old senses, so bewildering, so astonished at its own existence, that he could not credit it, and watched it as something apart, accidental, not to be trusted.

He slowly came to admit that Beethoven had partly become intelligible to him, but was the more inclined to think that Beethoven must be overrated to be so easily followed. This could not be called education, for he had never so much as listened. He had been thinking of other things. Mere mechanical repetition of certain sounds had stuck to his unconscious mind. Beethoven might have this power, but not Wagner, or not the Wagner later than Tannhäuser. Near forty years passed before he reached the Götterdämmerung.

No other sense awoke. His sense of line and color remained dull and far below an artist's level. His metaphysical sense did not spring into life to leap the bars of German expression into sympathy with the idealities of Kant and Hegel. He insisted that his faith in German thought and literature was exalted; he failed to approach German thought; he shed never a tear over the pages of Goethe and Schiller. When his father rashly ventured from time to time to write a word of common sense, the young man would listen to none, insisting that Berlin was the best of educations in the best of Germanies; yet when April came, and some genius suggested a tramp in Thüringen, his heart sang like a bird. He realized what a nightmare he had suffered, and made up his mind that, wherever else in the infinities of space and time he might seek for education, it should not be again in Berlin.

The tramp in Thuringen lasted four-and-twenty hours. By the end of the first walk his three companions—John Bancroft, James J. Higginson, and B. W. Crowninshield, all Boston and Harvard—were satisfied with what they had seen; and when they sat down where Goethe had written, "Warte nur! balde Rubest du auch!" the profundity of the counsel so affected them that they hired a wagon and drove to Weimar the same night. They were happy in the first fresh breath of leafless spring, and the beer was better than at Berlin; but all were equally in doubt why they had come to Germany, and none could say why they stayed. Adams stayed because he did not want to go home, and feared his father's patience might be exhausted if he asked to waste time elsewhere.

They could not think their education required a return to Berlin. A few days at Dresden satisfied them that Dresden was a better spot for general education than Berlin, and equally good for reading Civil Law. There was nothing to study in Dresden and no education to be gained, but the Sistine Madonna and the Correggios were famous; the theatre and opera were sometimes excellent; the Elbe was prettier than the Spree; and one could always fall back on the language. So he took a room in the household of the usual small government clerk with the usual plain daughters, and continued the study of the language. For the next eighteen months he pursued accidental education, since he could pursue no other; and by great good fortune, Europe and America were too busy with their own affairs to give much attention to his. Accidental education had every chance in its favor, especially because nothing came amiss.

Perhaps the chief obstacle to the youth's education, now that he had come of age, was his honesty—his simple-minded faith in his intentions. Even after Berlin had become a nightmare, he still persuaded himself that his German education was a success. He loved, or thought he loved, the people; but the Germany he loved was the eighteenth century which the Germans were ashamed of, and were destroying as fast as they could. Military Germany was his abhorrence. What he liked was the simple character; the good-natured sentiment; the musical and metaphysical abstraction; the blundering incapacity for practical affairs. Everyone looked on Germany as incapable of competing with France, England or America in organized energy. Until coal-power and railways were created, she was mediaeval by nature and geography, and this was what Adams, under the teachings of Carlyle and Lowell, liked.

He was in a fair way to do himself lasting harm, floundering between worlds passed and worlds coming, which had a habit of crushing men who stayed too long at the points of contact. Suddenly the Emperor Napoleon declared war on Austria and raised a confused point of morals in the mind of Europe. One morning the government clerk rushed in to consult a map and measure the distance from Milan to Dresden. The third Napoleon had reached Lombardy; only fifty or sixty years had passed since the first Napoleon began there.

An enlightened young American, with eighteenth-century tastes capped by fragments of a German education and the most excellent intentions, had to make up his mind about the moral value of these forces. France was the wicked spirit of moral politics, and whatever helped France must be evil. Austria was another evil spirit. Italy was the prize they disputed; for at least fifteen hundred years the question of sympathy had been put in cross-lights, and wisdom had been baffled by it. Better take sides first, and reason for the rest of life.

Not that Adams felt real doubt about sympathies or wishes; he had not been German long enough for befogging to that point. But the moment was decisive for political morals. Steam and electricity had brought new concentrations, or were making them necessary in the line of his moral principles—freedom, education, economic development—which required association with allies as doubtful as Napoleon III, and robberies with violence on a very extensive scale. As long as he could argue that his opponents were wicked, he could join in robbing and killing them without a qualm; but it might happen that the good were robbed. Education insisted on finding a moral foundation for robbery. He could hope to begin life in the character of no animal more moral than a monkey unless he could satisfy himself when and why robbery and murder were a virtue and duty. Education founded on mere self-interest was merely Guelph and Ghibelline over again—Machiavelli translated into American.

Luckily he had a sister much brighter than he ever was—though he thought himself a rather superior person—who, after marrying Charles Kuhn of Philadelphia, had come to Italy, and was hotly Italian. In July, 1859, at Thun, Henry joined them. Women commonly have a very positive moral sense; that which they will, is right; that which they reject, is wrong; and their will commonly ends by settling the moral. Mrs. Kuhn not only adored Italy; she cordially disliked Germany in all its varieties. She saw no gain in helping her brother to be Germanized, and wanted him much to be civilized. She was the first young woman he was ever intimate with—quick, sensitive, wilful, energetic, sympathetic, and intelligent enough to supply a score of men with ideas—and he was delighted to give her the reins, to let her drive him where she would. It was his first experiment in giving the reins to a woman, and he was so much pleased with the results that he never wanted to take them back. In after life he made a general law of experience—no woman had ever driven him wrong; no man had ever driven him right.

Nothing would satisfy Mrs. Kuhn but to go to the seat of war as soon as the armistice was declared. They crossed the St. Gothard and reached Milan, picturesque with every sort of uniform and every sign of war. To young Adams this first plunge into Italy passed Beethoven as a piece of accidental education: not a means of pursuing life, but one of the ends attained. It had but one defect—that of attainment.

Not satisfied with Milan, Mrs. Kuhn insisted on invading the enemy's country by the Stelvio Pass. The Valtellina showed war; Garibaldi's Cacciatori were the only visible inhabitants. Handsome young officers, delighted to talk of their battles, could not say whether the abhorred Austrian Jagers would let travellers through. After climbing what was said to be the finest carriage-pass in Europe, the carriage turned the last shoulder where the glacier of the Ortler Spitze tumbled down upon the road, and was driven directly up to the barricade—double lines of sentries stretching on either side up the mountains till the flash of gun barrels was lost in the flash of the snow. For accidental education the picture had its value; the earliest of such pictures count for most. The handsome blond officers of the Jagers were not to be beaten in courtesy by the olive-toned Cacciatori. The eternal woman had her way, and the barricade offered none. In fifteen minutes the carriage was rolling down to Mals, swarming with German soldiers and German fleas, and German atmosphere, of which, thanks to the glimpse of Italy, Adams never again felt quite the old confident charm.

Faithful to his self-contracted scheme of two winters in Civil Law, he went back to Dresden with a letter to the Frau Hofrathin von Reichenbach, in whose house Lowell and other Americans had pursued studies. In those days The Initials was a new book, and its cobweb charm over Munich gave a reflected light to Dresden. Young Adams had nothing to do but take fencing-lessons, visit galleries, and go to the theatre; but his social failure in the line of The Initials was humiliating, and the Frau Hofrathin sometimes laughed hugely at his helplessness before her society. Even the doctrine of accidental education broke down. There were no accidents in Dresden. As soon as the winter was over, he closed and locked the German door with a long breath of relief and took the road to Italy. He had pursued his education, as it pleased him, for eighteen months; in spite of infinite variety of new impressions packed into his mind, he knew no more, for his practical purposes, than the day he graduated. He had made no step towards a profession; was as ignorant as a schoolboy of society; unfit for any career in Europe and unfitted for any career in America; and he had not natural intelligence enough to see what a mess he had made of his education.

By twisting life to follow accidental and devious paths one might perhaps find use for accidental and devious knowledge, but nothing had been further from his mind than to become a tourist; and a mere tourist, and nothing else, he had become in April, 1860, when he joined his sister in Florence. His father had been in the right. Supposing his father asked what equivalent he had brought back for the time and money put into his experiment! The only possible answer would be: "Sir, I am a tourist!"

Meanwhile his father, sent to Congress by his Quincy district, was in the confusion of nominating candidates for the Presidential election. He supported Mr. Seward. The Republican Party was an unknown force; the Democratic Party was torn to pieces. No one could see far. For the time, the young man was safe from interference, and went on his way to take whatever chance fragments of education God or the devil was pleased to give him, for he knew no longer the good from the bad.

Perhaps the most useful purpose he served was that of his pen, for he wrote long letters to his brother Charles, who printed them in the Boston Courier; and the exercise was good. The habit of expression leads to the search for something to express; something remains as a residuum of the commonplace itself. Young men as a rule saw little in Italy, and in after life, when Adams began to learn what some men could see, he shrank into corners of shame at the thought that he had betrayed his inferiority as though it were his pride; but it was still the nearest approach he had made to an intelligent act.

For the rest, Italy was mostly an emotion, and the emotion naturally centred in Rome. The American parent, curiously enough, while bitterly hostile to Paris, seemed disposed to accept Rome as legitimate education, though abused; but to young men seeking education in a serious spirit, taking for granted that everything had a cause and that nature tended to an end, Rome was the most violent vice in the world, and Rome before 1870 was seductive beyond resistance. The lights and shadows were still mediaeval; the shadows breathed and glowed, full of soft forms felt by lost senses. No sand-blast of science had skinned off the epidermis of history, thought, and feeling. The pictures were uncleaned, the churches unrestored, the ruins unexcavated. Mediaeval Rome was sorcery. Rome was the worst spot on earth to teach nineteenth-century youth what to do with a twentieth-century world. One's emotions in Rome were one's private affair; they must be hurtful, else they could not have been so intense; and they were surely immoral, for no one could honestly read in the ruins any certain lesson but the just judgments of an outraged God against all the doings of man. This moral made Rome a gospel of anarchy and vice; yet it was, by common consent, the only spot that the young—of either sex and every race—passionately, perversely, wickedly loved.

Boys never see a conclusion; only on the edge of the grave can man conclude anything; but the first impulse given to the boy is apt to lead him for the rest of his life into conclusions he never dreamed of reaching. One looked idly enough at the Forum or at St. Peter's, but never forgot the look, and it never ceased reacting. Rome was not a beetle to be dissected and dropped; not a bad French novel to be read in a railway train and thrown out of the window after other bad French novels, the morals of which could never approach the immorality of Roman history. Rome was actual; it was England; it was going to be America. Rome could not be fitted into an orderly, Bostonian, systematic scheme of evolution. No law of progress applied. Not even time-sequences—the last refuge of helpless historians—had value for it. The Forum no more led to the Vatican than the Vatican to the Forum. Rienzi, Garibaldi, Tiberius Gracchus, Aurelian might be mixed in any relation of time and never lead to a sequence. The great word Evolution had not yet, in 1860, made a new religion of history, but the old religion had preached the same doctrine for a thousand years without finding in the entire history of Rome anything but flat contradiction.

Murray's Handbook had the grace to quote from Gibbon's Autobiography the words that started the Decline and Fall: "in the close of the evening, as I sat musing in the Church of the Zoccolanti or Franciscan Friars, while they were singing Vespers in the Temple of Jupiter, on the ruins of the Capitol." More than once Adams sat at sunset on the steps of Santa Maria di Ara Coeli, wondering that not an inch had been gained by Gibbon—or all the historians since—towards explaining the Fall. The mystery remained unsolved; the charm remained intact. Two great experiments of Western civilization had left there the chief monuments of their failure, and nothing proved that the city might not still survive to express the failure of a third.

The young man had no idea what he was doing. The thought of posing for a Gibbon never entered his mind. He was a tourist, even to the depths of his sub-consciousness, and it was well, for even the greatest cannot sit with dignity among the ruins of the Capitol unless they have something quite original to say. Tacitus could do it; so could Michael Angelo; and so, at a pinch, could Gibbon; but, in sum, none of them could say very much more than the tourist, who went on repeating to himself the eternal question:—Why! Why!! Why!!!—as his neighbor, the blind beggar, might do. No one ever had answered the question to the satisfaction of any one else; yet every one who had either head or heart felt that sooner or later he must make up his mind what answer to accept. Substitute the word America for the word Rome, and the question became personal.

Perhaps Henry learned something in Rome, though he never knew it. Rome dwarfs teachers. The greatest men of the age scarcely bore the test of posing with Rome for a background. One morning, chatting in Hamilton Wilde's studio, a middle-aged Englishman came in, excited, and told of his shock at coming unexpectedly on the guillotine near the Circus Maximus, where some criminal had been put to death an hour or two before. Only by slow degrees did Adams grapple the conviction that the victim of the shock was Robert Browning; and, on the background of the Circus Maximus, the Christian martyrs flaming as torches, and the morning's murderer on the block, Browning seemed rather in place, a middle-aged gentlemanly English Pippa Passes; while afterwards, in the light of Belgravia dinner-tables, he never made part of his background except by effacement. Browning might have sat with Gibbon among the ruins, and few Romans would have smiled.

Yet Browning never revealed the poetic depths of Saint Francis; William Story could not touch the secret of Michael Angelo; Mommsen hardly said all that one felt by instinct in the lives of Cicero and Caesar. They taught what, as a rule, needed no teaching, the lessons of a rather cheap imagination and cheaper politics. Rome was a bewildering complex of ideas, experiments, ambitions, energies; without her, the Western world was pointless and fragmentary; she gave heart and unity to it all; yet Gibbon might have gone on for the whole century, and no one would have passed, capable of telling him what it meant. Perhaps it meant nothing.

So ended the happiest month of May life had yet offered, fading beyond the past somewhere into abstract time, grotesquely out of place with the Berlin scheme or a Boston future. Adams explained to himself that he was absorbing knowledge. He would have put it better had he said that knowledge was absorbing him. He was passive. In spite of swarming impressions he knew no more when he left Rome than when he entered it. As a marketable object, his value was less.

He went on to Naples, and there, in the hot June, heard rumors that Garibaldi and his thousand were about to attack Palermo. Calling on the American Minister, Chandler of Pennsylvania, he was kindly treated, not for his merit but for his name, and sent as bearer of despatches to Captain Palmer of the Iroquois. He went to Palermo in a government transport filled with fleas, commanded by a charming Prince Caracciolo, and told all about it to the Boston Courier; but of its bearing on education the Courier did not speak. He himself would have liked to know whether it had any bearing, and what was its value as a post-graduate course. Loosely, the tourist called it knowledge of men, but it was just the reverse; it was knowledge of one's ignorance of men.

Captain Palmer took him with the officers to make an evening call on Garibaldi, whom they found in the Senate House towards sunset, at supper with his picturesque and piratic staff, in the full noise and color of the Palermo revolution. Garibaldi left the table, and, sitting at the window, had a few words with Captain Palmer and young Adams. At that moment he was certainly the most serious of the doubtful energies in the world; even then society was dividing between banker and anarchist; one or the other Garibaldi must serve. Himself a typical anarchist, sure to overshadow Europe and alarm empires bigger than Naples, his success depended on his mind.

Adams had the chance to look this sphinx in the eyes, and for five minutes to watch him like a wild animal, at the moment of his greatest achievement and most splendid action. One saw a quiet-featured, quiet-voiced man in a red flannel shirt; absolutely impervious; sympathetic and simple, perhaps childlike, but betraying no thought. In his own eyes he might be a Napoleon or a Spartacus; in the hands of Cavour he might become a Condottiere; in the eyes of history he might be only the vigorous player in a game he did not understand. The student was none the wiser. This compound nature of patriot and pirate had illumined Italian history from the beginning, and was no more intelligible to itself than to a young American who had no experience in double natures. In the end, if the Autobiography tells truth, Garibaldi saw and said that he had not understood his own acts; that he had been an instrument; that he had served the purposes of the class he least wanted to help; yet in 1860 he thought himself the revolution, Napoleonic, anarchic, with unbounded ambition. What should a young Bostonian have made of a character like this, internally alive with childlike fancies, externally quiet, simple, almost innocent; uttering with conviction the commonplaces of popular politics; and never betraying a thought?

Precisely this class of mind was to be the toughest problem of Adams's practical life, but he could never make anything of it. The lesson of Garibaldi, as education, seemed to teach the extreme complexity of extreme simplicity; but one could have learned this from a glow-worm. One did not need the vivid recollection of the low-voiced, simple-mannered, seafaring captain of Genoese adventurers and Sicilian brigands, supping in the July heat and Sicilian dirt and revolutionary clamor among the barricaded streets of Palermo, merely to remember that simplicity is complex.

He came north to stumble over others, less picturesque but nearer. He squandered two or three months on Paris. From the first he had avoided Paris and wanted no French influence; he disapproved of France in the lump; the Empire and the Emperor particularly; most of all the French mind. To save himself the trouble of a long list of dislikes, he shut them figuratively out of his life. France was not serious, and he was not serious in going there.

He did this in good faith, obeying his teachers; but the curious result followed that, being in no way responsible for the French and sincerely disapproving them, he felt quite at liberty to enjoy to the full everything he disapproved. Several thousand Americans passed much of their time there on this understanding. They sought to take share in every function open to approach as they sought tickets to the opera, because they were not a part of it. Adams did like the rest. He tried to acquire a few idioms, without aspiring to master a subjunctive; succeeded better in acquiring a modest taste for Bordeaux and Burgundy and one or two sauces; for the Trois Freres Provencaux and Voisin's and Philippe's and the Cafe Anglais; for the Palais Royal Theatre, and the Varietes and the Gymnase; for the Brohans and Bressant, Rose Cheri and Gil Perez, and other lights of the stage. Life was amusing; Paris rapidly became familiar; in a month or six weeks he forgot even to disapprove; but he studied nothing, entered no society, and made no acquaintance. Accidental education went far in Paris, and one picked up a deal of knowledge that might become useful; perhaps, after all, the three months there might serve better purpose than the twenty-one months elsewhere; but he did not intend it, did not think it, and looked at it as a momentary and frivolous vacation before going home to fit himself for life. Therewith, after staying as long as he could and spending all the money he dared, he started with mixed emotions but no education, for home.

When, forty years afterwards, Henry Adams looked back over his adventures in search of knowledge, he asked himself whether fortune or fate had ever dealt its cards so wildly to any of his known antecessors as when it led him to begin the study of law and to vote for Abraham Lincoln on the same day. He dropped back on Quincy like a lump of lead; he rebounded like a football, tossed into space by an unknown energy which played with all his generation as a cat plays with mice. Not one man in America wanted the Civil War, or expected or intended it. A small minority wanted secession. The vast majority wanted to go on with their occupations in peace. Not one, however clever or learned, guessed what happened.

Fresh from Europe and chaos of another sort, he plunged at once into a lurid atmosphere of politics. His past melted away. The prodigal was welcomed home; his father hinted only at some shade of prodigality by quietly inviting his son to act as private secretary during the winter in Washington, as though any young man who could afford to throw away two winters on the Civil Law could afford to read Blackstone for another winter without a master. November at Quincy had from earliest childhood been the least gay of seasons; yet a Quincy November seemed temperate before the chill of a Boston January. The November of 1860 stood apart: although no one believed in civil war, the air reeked of it; Republicans, as Wide-Awakes, paraded in ranks of torches, their air not that of innocence as they wound down the hillside to the Old House where Mr. Adams, their Member of Congress, received them.

Profoundly ignorant, anxious, and curious, the young man packed again and started for Washington with his family. Ten years had changed little. As in 1800 and 1850, so in 1860, the same rude colony camped in the same forest, with the same unfinished Greek temples for work rooms, and sloughs for roads. The Government had an air of social instability and incompleteness that seemed to support the right of secession in theory as in fact; right or wrong, secession was likely to be easy where there was so little to secede from. The Union was a sentiment, but not much more, and about the Capitol in December the sentiment was chiefly hostile. John Adams was better off in Philadelphia in 1776 than his great-grandson in Washington in 1860.

Patriotism threw a halo over the Continental Congress; over the close of the Thirty-sixth Congress none could be thrown by any one who saw it. Of all the crowd swarming in Washington that winter, young Adams was surely among the most ignorant and helpless, but the knowledge of everybody about him was hardly greater than his own. Mr. Sumner was fond of saying after Oxenstiern: “Quantula sapientia mundus regitur!” Oxenstiern talked of a world that wanted wisdom; Adams found himself seeking education in a world that seemed to him both unwise and ignorant. The Southern secessionists were unbalanced in mind—victims of hallucination—haunted by suspicion, idées fixes, morbid excitement; but this was not all. They were stupendously ignorant of the world. As a class, the cotton-planters were mentally one-sided, ill-balanced, provincial to a degree rarely known. On them the new fountains of power had poured wealth and slaves that acted like oil on flame. They gave a young student his first object-lesson of excess of power held by inadequate hands.

In 1860 this was paradox. Southern statesmen were regarded as standards of statesmanship, and such standards barred education. From such contradictions what was a young man to learn? He could learn nothing but cross-purpose. The planter-gentleman had nothing to teach, except warning. Even as example to be avoided he defied reason too glaringly to help a reasonable being. Thus, at one sweep, the whole field of instruction south of the Potomac was shut off; overshadowed by cotton-planters, from whom one could learn nothing but bad temper, bad manners, poker, and treason.

Perforce, the student was thrown back on Northern precept and example; first of all, on his New England surroundings. Republican houses were few in Washington; Mr. and Mrs. Adams aimed to create a social centre for New Englanders at the Markoe house on I Street. The private secretary began to learn his social duties; the political were confined to committee-rooms and lobbies. The Southern type was to be avoided; the New England type was one’s self—sane and steady men, well-balanced, educated, free from meanness or intrigue, bearing the stamp of Harvard College. Their strength was poise which almost amounted to a defect; they offered no target for love or hate; even as machines, their motion seemed never accelerated. One knew the character to the core; one was it.

There remained the Central and Western States: a few figures—Preston King, Henry Winter Davis, Owen Lovejoy, men born with social faculty; Henry J. Raymond, a man of the world. The average Congressman was civil, but had nothing to ask except offices, and nothing to offer but the views of his district. The average Senator was more reserved, and, excepting one or two genial natures, handicapped by his own importance. Study it as one might, the hope of education narrowed to the possible influence of only two men—Sumner and Seward.

Sumner was then fifty. After his Brooks injuries his nervous system never quite recovered its tone; perhaps ten years of solitary existence as Senator did more. No man can serve ten years as schoolmaster, priest, or Senator, and remain fit for anything else. Dogmatic stations fix a stiffness of attitude, as though they mesmerized the subject. Even among Senators there were degrees—from South Carolinian brutality to the massive Webster, Benton, Clay, or Sumner himself—until in extreme cases, like Conkling, it became Shakespearian and bouffe—Malvolio. Sumner had become dogmatic like the rest, but his qualities warranted dogmatism. He justly thought his services and sacrifices, education, oratorical power, political experience, representative character at the head of New England, and knowledge of the world, made him the most important member of the Senate; and no Senator had more thoroughly saturated himself with its spirit and temper. Senators seldom proclaim inferiority to each other, and like still less to be told of it. Even the greatest inspired little personal affection. Sumner had rivals who held his judgment in no high esteem; one was Senator Seward. The two men would have disliked each other by instinct had they lived in different planets. Each was created to exasperate the other; the virtues of one were the faults of his rival; that the public service must suffer was certain. What were its sufferings compared with the risks run by a young mosquito—a private secretary—trying to buzz admiration in the ears of each, unaware that each would slap at him for belonging to the other? Innocent and unsuspicious beyond nursery permission, the private secretary courted both.

Private secretaries are servants whose business is to serve sources of power. The first news of a professional kind, on reaching Washington, was that Mr. Lincoln had selected Mr. Seward for Secretary of State, and that Seward was to be the medium for communicating his wishes to his followers. Every young man naturally accepted Mr. Lincoln’s wishes as orders, the more because the new President was likely to need all the help that several million young men could give, if they counted on having any President at all to serve. One waited impatiently for the first meeting with the new Secretary.

Governor Seward had long been a family friend; since 1849 his leadership had separated him from the Free Soil remnant; the fierce heat that welded the Republican Party in 1856 melted such barriers, and in 1859 he renewed his intimacy, lost no chance of forcing Mr. Adams to the front. A few days after their arrival in December, he came to dinner alone, as one of the family, and the private secretary watched the man who disposed of his future: a slouching, slender figure; a head like a wise macaw; a beaked nose; shaggy eyebrows; unorderly hair and clothes; hoarse voice; offhand manner; free talk; a perpetual cigar. A new type—western New York—double, political and personal; complex, because the political had become nature, and none could tell which was mask and which features. Among friends he seemed to throw off restraint; in the world he threw it off for effect. He loathed pomposity and enjoyed a joke; how much was nature and how much mask, he was himself too simple to know. Underneath he was conventional by Albany’s conventions. Politicians called it unconventionality; Bostonians called it provincial; Henry thought it charming. From the first sight he loved the Governor, who, though sixty, had the youth of his sympathies. He noticed that Seward was never petty or personal; his talk was large; he generalized; he never posed for statesmanship; he did not require an attitude of prayer; and—almost singular—he had some means of producing the effect of unselfishness.

Superficially Seward and Mr. Adams were contrasts; essentially much alike. The Puritan character, thought rigid, could be supple enough; in Massachusetts all the Adamses had been attacked in succession as no better than political mercenaries. Hildreth echoed the charge that treachery was hereditary in the family. An Adams had to be thick-skinned, armed to return such attentions; but all must admit they subordinated local to national interests, and would continue to do so when forced to choose. C. F. Adams was sure to do what his father had done, as his father had followed John Adams, and no doubt earned his epithets.

The inevitable followed, as a child fresh from the nursery should have foreseen; but the young man never dreamed. He made no pretence of guessing motives; he knew only his own infantile ignorance and innocent good-faith. Rightly or wrongly the new President and his chief advisers decided that, before they could administer the Government, they must make sure of a government to administer, and that this chance depended on Virginia. The winter’s ascendancy wavered between the effort of the cotton States to drag Virginia out, and of the new President to keep Virginia in. Seward, representing the Administration in the Senate, took the lead; Mr. Adams took the lead in the House; the party united on its tactics. In offering concessions to the border States, they ran the risk—or incurred the certainty—of dividing their own party, and took it with open eyes. As Seward himself, in his gruff way, said at dinner, after he and Mr. Adams had made their speeches: “If there’s no secession now, you and I are ruined.”

They won their game. The sudden arrival of Mr. Lincoln on February 23, and the language of his inaugural, were the final term of the winter’s tactics, and closed the private secretary’s interest. Perhaps he felt more interest in another private secretary, of his own age, a young man named John Hay, who lighted on LaFayette Square at the same moment. Friends are born, not made. From the first slight meeting, he recognized Hay as a friend, and never lost sight of him; but, for the moment, his own task ended on March 4 when Hay’s began. The winter’s anxieties were shifted upon new shoulders, and Henry gladly turned back to Blackstone. He had tried to make himself useful, acting in secret as newspaper correspondent, cultivating a large acquaintance, even haunting ballrooms where the simple, old-fashioned Southern tone was pleasant in an atmosphere of conspiracy and treason. The sum was next to nothing for education, because no one could teach; all were as ignorant as himself; none knew what should be done or how; all were more bent on asking than answering questions. The mass of ignorance in Washington was lighted up by no ray of knowledge. Society, from top to bottom, broke down.

From this law there was no exception, unless perhaps old General Winfield Scott, the only military figure that looked equal to the crisis. No one else either looked it, or was it, by nature or training. Had young Adams’s life hung on the correctness of his estimate of the new President, he would have lost. He saw Mr. Lincoln but once, at the melancholy Inaugural Ball: a long, awkward figure; a plain, ploughed face; a mind, absent in part, and in part worried by white kid gloves; features that expressed neither self-satisfaction nor other familiar Americanism, but rather the same painful sense of needing education that tormented a private secretary; above all a lack of apparent force. Any private secretary fit for his business would have thought, as Adams did, that no man living needed so much education as the new President, but that all the education he could get would not be enough.

As far as one could see, no one in Washington was fitted for his duties; or rather, no duties in March were fitted for the duties in April. The few who thought they knew were more in error than those who knew nothing. Only one man in Adams’s reach seemed supremely fitted by knowledge and experience to be adviser and friend: Senator Sumner; and there, in fact, the young man’s education began; there it ended.

Going over the experience long after all the great actors were dead, he struggled to see where he had blundered. In the effort to make acquaintances, he lost friends; could he have helped it? He had necessarily followed Seward and his father; he took for granted that his business was obedience, discipline, and silence; he supposed the party to require it, and the crisis to overrule personal doubts. He was thunderstruck to learn that Senator Sumner privately denounced the course, regarded Mr. Adams as betraying the principles of his life, and broke off relations with his family.

Many a shock was he to meet in a long life near politics; but the profoundest lessons are not the lessons of reason; they are sudden strains that permanently warp the mind. He cared little for the point in discussion; was willing to admit that Sumner might be right, though in all great emergencies he commonly found every one more or less wrong; he liked lofty moral principle; he respected Sumner; but the shock opened a chasm that never closed, and as long as life lasted, he found himself invariably taking for granted, as a political instinct—without waiting further experiment—as he took for granted that arsenic poisoned—the rule that a friend in power is a friend lost.

On his own score, he never admitted the rupture, and never exchanged a word with Sumner on the subject, then or afterwards, but his education—for good or bad—made an enormous stride. He was looking at hundreds of Southern gentlemen who believed themselves honest, but who seemed engaged in the plainest breach of faith and the blackest secret conspiracy; yet they did not disturb his education; history told of little else; not one rebel defection—not even Lee’s—cost him a personal pang; but Sumner’s struck home.

This, then, was the result of the attempt at education down to March 4, 1861; and it was hardly what he wanted. Washington offered education, but not the kind that led to good. The process Matthew Arnold described as wandering between two worlds, one dead, the other powerless to be born, helps nothing. Even before the traitors had flown, the vultures descended in swarms that darkened the ground, and tore the carrion of political patronage into fragments and gobbets of fat and lean on the very steps of the White House. Not a man there knew what his task was to be, or was fitted for it; every one without exception was to learn his business at the cost of the public. Lincoln, Seward, Sumner, and the rest could give no help; they knew less than he; within six weeks they were all to be taught their duties by the uprising of such as he, and their education was to cost a million lives and ten thousand million dollars, more or less, before the country could recover its balance and movement. Henry was a helpless victim, and, like all the rest, he could only wait for he knew not what, to send him he knew not where.

With the close of the session, his own functions ended. Ceasing to be private secretary, he knew not what else to do but return with his father and mother to Boston in mid-March, and, with childlike docility, sit down at a desk in the law-office of Horace Gray in Court Street, to begin again: “My Lords and Gentlemen”; dozing after a two o’clock dinner, or waking to discuss politics with the future Justice. There, in ordinary times, he would have remained for life; his attempt at education in treason having, like all the rest, disastrously failed.

Hardly a week passed when the newspapers announced that President Lincoln had selected Charles Francis Adams as his Minister to England. Once more, silently, Henry put Blackstone back on its shelf. As Friar Bacon's head sententiously announced many centuries before: Time had passed! The Civil Law lasted a brief day; the Common Law prolonged its shadowy existence for a week. The law, altogether, as path of education, vanished in April, 1861, leaving a million young men planted in the mud of a lawless world, to begin a new life without education at all.

Mr. Adams once more intimated that he thought himself entitled to the services of one of his sons, and indicated Henry as the only one who could be spared. Henry packed his trunk again without a word. Ridiculous as he knew himself about to be, he was less ridiculous than his betters. He was not a vulture of carrion—patronage. He knew that his father's appointment was the result of Governor Seward's personal friendship; he did not then know that Senator Sumner had opposed it, but he could have supplied proofs enough, the strongest being that, in his opinion, Mr. Adams had chosen a private secretary far more unfit than his chief.
